<#sep#>1}(1-e^{-t})-{\underline {et))}$ and the constant of integration ${\displaystyle B_{1))$ must be obtained from inner-outer matching.

Notice, the intuitive idea for matching of taking the limits i.e. ${\displaystyle \lim _{\tau \rightarrow \infty }y_{\mathrm {I} }=\lim _{t\to 0}y_{\mathrm {O} },\,}$ doesn't apply at this level. This is simply because the underlined term doesn't converge to a limit. The methods to follow in these types of cases are either to go for a) method of an intermediate variable or using b) the Van-Dyke matching rule. The former method is cumbersome and works always whereas the Van-Dyke matching rule is easy to implement but with limited applicability. A concrete boundary value problem having all the essential ingredients is the following.

Consider the boundary value problem

${\displaystyle \varepsilon y''-x^{2}y'-y=1,\quad y(0)=y(1)=1}$

The conventional outer expansion ${\displaystyle y_{\mathrm {O} }=y_{0}+\varepsilon y_{1}+...,}$ gives

${\displaystyle y_{0}=\alpha e^{1/x}-1}$, where ${\displaystyle \alpha }$ must be obtained from matching.

The problem has boundary layers both on the left and on the right. The left boundary layer near ${\displaystyle 0}$ has a thickness ${\displaystyle \varepsilon ^{1/2))$ whereas the right boundary layer near ${\displaystyle 1}$ has thickness ${\displaystyle \varepsilon }$. Let us first calculate the solution on the left boundary layer by rescaling ${\displaystyle X=x/\varepsilon ^{1/2},\;Y=y}$, then the differential equation to satisfy on the left is

${\displaystyle Y''-\varepsilon ^{1/2}X^{2}Y'-Y=1,\quad Y(0)=1}$

and accordingly, we assume an expansion ${\displaystyle Y^{l}=Y_{0}^{l}+\varepsilon ^{1/2}Y_{1/2}^{l}+...,}$.

The ${\displaystyle {\mathcal {O))(1)}$ inhomogeneous condition on the left provides us the reason to start the expansion at ${\displaystyle {\mathcal {O))(1)}$. The leading order solution is ${\displaystyle Y_{0}^{l}=2e^{-X}-1}$.

This with ${\displaystyle 1-1}$ van-Dyke matching gives ${\displaystyle \alpha =0}$.

Let us now calculate the solution on the right rescaling ${\displaystyle X=(1-x)/\varepsilon ,\;Y=y}$, then the differential equation to satisfy on the right is

${\displaystyle Y''+(1-2\varepsilon X+\varepsilon ^{2}X^{2})Y'-\varepsilon Y=\varepsilon ,\quad Y(1)=1}$,

and accordingly, we assume an expansion

${\displaystyle Y^{r}=Y_{0}^{r}+\varepsilon ^{1/2}Y_{1/2}^{r}+...}$.

The ${\displaystyle {\mathcal {O))(1)}$ inhomogeneous condition on the right provides us the reason to start the expansion at ${\displaystyle {\mathcal {O))(1)}$. The leading order solution is ${\displaystyle Y_{0}^{r}=(1-B)+Be^{-X))$. This with ${\displaystyle 1-1}$ van-Dyke matching gives ${\displaystyle B=2}$. Proceeding in a similar fashion if we calculate the higher order-corrections we get the solutions as

${\displaystyle Y^{l}=2e^{-X}-1+\varepsilon ^{1/2}e^{-X}({\frac {X^{3)){3))+{\frac {X^{2)){2))+{\frac {X}{2)))+{\mathcal {O))(\varepsilon )...,\quad X={\frac {x}{\varepsilon ^{1/2))))$.

${\displaystyle y\equiv -1}$.

${\displaystyle Y^{r}=2e^{-X}-1+2\varepsilon e^{-X}(X+X^{2})+{\mathcal {O))(\varepsilon ^{2})...,\quad X={\frac {1-x}{\varepsilon ))}$.

### Composite solution

To obtain our final, matched, composite solution, valid on the whole domain, one popular method is the uniform method. In this method, we add the inner and outer approximations and subtract their overlapping value, ${\displaystyle \,y_{\mathrm {overlap} ))$, which would otherwise be counted twice. The overlapping value is the outer limit of the inner boundary layer solution, and the inner limit of the outer solution; these limits were above found to equal ${\displaystyle e}$. Therefore, the final approximate solution to this boundary value problem is,

${\displaystyle y(t)=y_{\mathrm {I} }+y_{\mathrm {O} }-y_{\mathrm {overlap} }=e\left({1-e^{-t/\varepsilon ))\right)+e^{1-t}-e=e\left({e^{-t}-e^{-t/\varepsilon ))\right).\,}$

Note that this expression correctly reduces to the expressions for ${\displaystyle y_{\mathrm {I} ))$ and ${\displaystyle y_{\mathrm {O} ))$ when ${\displaystyle t}$ is O(${\displaystyle \varepsilon }$) and O(1), respectively.

### Accuracy

Convergence of approximations. Approximations and exact solutions, which are indistinguishable at this scale, are shown for various ${\displaystyle \varepsilon }$. The outer solution is also shown. Note that since the boundary layer becomes narrower with decreasing ${\displaystyle \varepsilon }$, the approximations converge to the outer solution pointwise, but not uniformly, almost everywhere.

This final solution satisfies the problem's original differential equation (shown by substituting it and its derivatives into the original equation). Also, the boundary conditions produced by this final solution match the values given in the problem, up to a constant multiple. This implies, due to the uniqueness of the solution, that the matched asymptotic solution is identical to the exact solution up to a constant multiple. This is not necessarily always the case, any remaining terms should go to zero uniformly as ${\displaystyle \varepsilon \rightarrow 0}$.

Not only does our solution successfully approximately solve the problem at hand, it closely approximates the problem's exact solution. It happens that this particular problem is easily found to have exact solution

${\displaystyle y(t)={\frac {e^{-t}-e^{-t/\varepsilon )){e^{-1}-e^{-1/\varepsilon ))},\,}$

which has the same form as the approximate solution, by the multiplying constant. The approximate solution is the first term in a binomial expansion of the exact solution in powers of ${\displaystyle e^{1-1/\varepsilon ))$.

### Location of boundary layer

Conveniently, we can see that the boundary layer, where ${\displaystyle y'}$ and ${\displaystyle y''}$ are large, is near ${\displaystyle t=0}$, as we supposed earlier. If we had supposed it to be at the other endpoint and proceeded by making the rescaling ${\displaystyle \tau =(1-t)/\varepsilon }$, we would have found it impossible to satisfy the resulting matching condition. For many problems, this kind of trial and error is the only way to determine the true location of the boundary layer.[2]

## Harder problems

The problem above is a simple example because it is a single equation with only one dependent variable, and there is one boundary layer in the solution. Harder problems may contain several co-dependent variables in a system of several equations, and/or with several boundary and/or interior layers in the solution.

It is often desirable to find more terms in the asymptotic expansions of both the outer and the inner solutions. The appropriate form of these expansions is not always clear: while a power-series expansion in ${\displaystyle \varepsilon }$ may work, sometimes the appropriate form involves fractional powers of ${\displaystyle \varepsilon }$, functions such as ${\displaystyle \varepsilon \log \varepsilon }$, et cetera. As in the above example, we will obtain outer and inner expansions with some coefficients which must be determined by matching.[6]

## Second-order differential equations

### Schrödinger-like second-order differential equations

A<#sep#>-

#include <bits/stdc++.h>
using namespace std;

int main() {
    ios::sync_with_stdio(false);
    cin.tie(nullptr);

    int n, m;
    cin >> n >> m;

    vector<vector<pair<int, int>>> adj(n + 1);
    vector<pair<int, int>> edges(m + 1);

    for (int i = 1; i <= m; ++i) {
        int u, v;
        cin >> u >> v;
        edges[i] = {u, v};
        adj[u].emplace_back(v, i);
        adj[v].emplace_back(u, i);
    }

    vector<bool> visited_edges(m + 1, false);
    vector<int> disc(n + 1, -1), low(n + 1, -1);
    vector<vector<int>> components;
    stack<tuple<int, int, int>> edge_stack;

    for (int u = 1; u <= n; ++u) {
        if (disc[u] == -1) {
            stack<tuple<int, int, vector<pair<int, int>>::iterator>> dfs_stack;
            dfs_stack.emplace(u, -1, adj[u].begin());
            disc[u] = low[u] = 0;

            while (!dfs_stack.empty()) {
                int v = get<0>(dfs_stack.top());
                int parent_v = get<1>(dfs_stack.top());
                auto &it = get<2>(dfs_stack.top());
                bool is_root = (parent_v == -1);

                if (disc[v] == -1) {
                    static int time = 0;
                    disc[v] = low[v] = ++time;
                }

                bool found = false;
                for (; it != adj[v].end(); ++it) {
                    auto [w, idx] = *it;
                    if (w == parent_v || visited_edges[idx]) continue;

                    if (disc[w] == -1) {
                        visited_edges[idx] = true;
                        edge_stack.emplace(v, w, idx);
                        dfs_stack.pop();
                        dfs_stack.emplace(v, parent_v, next(it));
                        dfs_stack.emplace(w, v, adj[w].begin());
                        disc[w] = low[w] = 0;
                        found = true;
                        break;
                    } else if (disc[w] < disc[v]) {
                        visited_edges[idx] = true;
                        edge_stack.emplace(v, w, idx);
                        low[v] = min(low[v], disc[w]);
                    }
                }

                if (!found) {
                    dfs_stack.pop();

                    if (!is_root) {
                        for (auto [to, idx] : adj[v]) {
                            if (to == parent_v || !visited_edges[idx] || disc[to] <= disc[v]) continue;
                            low[v] = min(low[v], low[to]);
                        }

                        if (low[v] >= disc[parent_v]) {
                            vector<int> comp;
                            while (true) {
                                auto [u1, u2, eidx] = edge_stack.top();
                                edge_stack.pop();
                                comp.push_back(eidx);
                                if ((u1 == parent_v && u2 == v) || (u1 == v && u2 == parent_v)) break;
                            }
                            components.push_back(comp);
                        }
                        low[parent_v] = min(low[parent_v], low[v]);
                    } else {
                        vector<int> comp;
                        while (!edge_stack.empty()) {
                            auto [u1, u2, eidx] = edge_stack.top();
                            edge_stack.pop();
                            comp.push_back(eidx);
                            if (u1 == v || u2 == v) break;
                        }
                        if (!comp.empty()) {
                            components.push_back(comp);
                        }
                    }
                }
            }
        }
    }

    vector<bool> in_ans(m + 1, false);
    for (auto &comp : components) {
        unordered_map<int, int> deg;
        for (int idx : comp) {
            auto [u, v] = edges[idx];
            deg[u]++;
            deg[v]++;
        }
        bool is_cycle = true;
        for (auto [u, cnt] : deg) {
            if (cnt != 2) {
                is_cycle = false;
                break;
            }
        }
        if (is_cycle) {
            for (int idx : comp) {
                in_ans[idx] = true;
            }
        }
    }

    vector<int> ans;
    for (int i = 1; i <= m; ++i) {
        if (in_ans[i]) {
            ans.push_back(i);
        }
    }

    cout << ans.size() << "\n";
    for (int i = 0; i < ans.size(); ++i) {
        if (i > 0) cout << " ";
        cout << ans[i];
    }
    cout << "\n";

\]<#sep#>human: Solve a Tron Game
Tron is a great game, and if you haven't played it the internet has MANY online versions available.
A fun challenge is to try to write an AI that plays Tron, which is normally much better than any human, thanks to their frame perfect dodging.
Even with their perfect dodging of walls, they still get themselves trapped easily.
Your task, is to write a script that when fed a map containing a single tron bike, that may already be partially filled, and output the actions required to fill it as much as possible.
The Task
From the letter p in the input, find the best moves to fill the most space in the maze, in as few iterations as possible, then output as a string of r(ight)u(p)l(eft)d(own), describing that path.
If you attempt to move into wall, your run is ended.
if you attempt to move where you have already been, your run is ended.
if your run contains an unexpected character, it is ended at that character.
Filling space refers to passing over it in your run. Your starting position is counted. 
Input / Output
Your input will be through any valid method,
A maze, consisting of the characters # p (Pounds (0x23), Literal space (0x20) and the letter p lowercase (0x70))
one of the following forms

A single multiline string
An array of strings, split by lines
An array of arrays, each containing a single character

You must output exactly

A string or array of strings containing only the characters rlud case sensitive, describing your path through the maze.

You may use any reasonable permutation of these input and output format as well, such as bytes instead of characters.
Test Cases
Straight Forward.
Input:
#######
#p    #
#######

Example Output: rrrr
A Turn.
Input:
#######
#p    #
##### #
    # #
    ###

Example Output: rrrrdd
Box Filling.

#     #
#######

Example Output: rrrrdlllldrrrr
Complicated

    ###

Unfillable
Input:
 ########
 #p     #
 #     ##
###### #
#      #
##     #
 #######

Scoring
Your score is a combination of how fast your code runs and how many squares you fill of all the test cases.
Score is Total successful moves across Test Cases / Algorithmic Complexity Across Test Cases
So if your Algorithm has a complexity of O(a*b) Where 'a' is Maze Width and 'b' is Maze Height, then your Algorithm Complexity Total is (TestCase1Width * TestCase1Height) + (TestCase2Width * TestCase2Height)...
See More on Big O Notation
Highest score Wins!
Notes

The input will always be an enclosed surrounding the p
There will always be atleast 1 space to move from the start
The area may not always be possible to fill completely.
Your code should still work competitively on a random valid input. That is, do not cater just to the test cases.

var storedMap = "";
 function restoreMap(){
  document.getElementById("maze").value = storedMap;
 }
 function trymaze(){
  var score = "";
  var maze = document.getElementById("maze");
  storedMap = maze.value;
  var commands = document.getElementById("methodin");
  var converts = {r:{r:"─",u:"┌",d:"└",l:"─"},u:{r:"┘",u:"│",d:"│",l:"└"},d:{r:"┐",u:"│",d:"│",l:"┌"},l:{r:"─",u:"┐",d:"┘",l:"─"}}
  var dirs={l:{x:-1,y:0},u:{x:0,y:-1},r:{x:1,y:0},d:{x:0,y:1}};
  var last = "";
  var boxes = commands.value.replace(/./g, function(c){
   if(last==""){
    last = c;
    return {r:"╶",u:"╵",d:"╷",l:"╴"}[c];
   }
   var s = converts[c][last];
   last = c;
   return s;
  })
  var mMaze = maze.value.replace("p"," ").split("\n");
  var pp = maze.value.indexOf("p")
  var y = (maze.value.substring(1,pp).match(/\n/g)||[]).length;
  var x = ((maze.value.match(/.*p/)||[""])[0].length)-1;
  var path = commands.value.split("");
  for(s in path){
   tx = x + dirs[path[s]].x;
   ty = y + dirs[path[s]].y;
   mMaze[y] = mMaze[y].replaceAt(x, boxes[s]);
   if(!mMaze[ty]){
    alert("NO MAZE SEGMENT "+ty);
    break;
   }
   if(mMaze[ty].substring(tx,tx+1)!=" "){
    alert("HIT A WALL: "+tx+" "+ty+" \""+mMaze[ty].substring(tx,tx+1)+"\"")
    break;
   }
   score++;
   x=tx;
   y=ty;
   mMaze[y] = mMaze[y].replaceAt(x, "O");
  }
  if(document.getElementById("score").value){
   document.getElementById("score").value = document.getElementById("score").value + ", " + score;
  }else{
   document.getElementById("score").value = score;
  }
  maze.value = mMaze.join("\n");
 }

 String.prototype.replaceAt=function(index, character) {
     return this.substr(0, index) + character + this.substr(index+character.length);
 }

gpt: <think> Okay, so I need to solve this Tron game problem. Let's see... The task is to write an AI that, given a maze with a tron bike's starting position (the 'p' character), finds the path that allows the bike to move as much as possible, collecting the maximum number of squares, and output the directions as 'r', 'u', 'l', 'd' in a string. The path can't hit walls or previously visited squares, obviously, and if the path has invalid characters, it also ends.

Hmm. The main goal is to find the longest possible path starting at the p, without getting stuck until there's no possible move left. Since this is for a Tron game, the bike leaves a trail behind, so every move must be into an unoccupied space. So, it's a longest path problem on a grid.

Longest path is NP-hard, but given that the mazes here may not be that big, maybe an approach like BFS or DFS with backtracking could work, but for larger mazes, it's going to be slow. However, the problem mentions that the solution should work competitively on random inputs, so maybe it's expecting something more efficient.

Wait, but the scoring is based on the total successful moves divided by algorithmic complexity, so higher is better. So for example, if an algorithm runs in O(N*M) (time proportional to maze area multiplied each time) over the test cases, that's worse than O(N + M), which could be better. So I need to prioritize an efficient algorithm even more.

Let me think. The longest path problem is indeed difficult, but since the game is constrained by the trails, perhaps a greedy approach that always chooses the direction leading to the next possibility with the most options first might help. Because sometimes choosing a path that leaves more avenues open later can lead to a longer path.

Alternatively, maybe a BFS-based approach where we track all possible paths so that we can find the longest one. But the issue is that for mazes with large areas, this would be computationally expensive. For example, in a maze with 100x100 squares, the path length could be up to 10,000, and keeping all possible states would be impossible unless pruned effectively.

Hmm, perhaps the key is to use memoization or dynamic programming to track for each position and direction the farthest path possible. But I'm not sure.

<#sep#> the asymptotic expansions of both the outer and the inner solutions. The appropriate form of these expansions is not always clear: while a power-series expansion in ${\displaystyle \varepsilon }$ may work, sometimes the appropriate form involves fractional powers of ${\displaystyle \varepsilon }$, functions such as ${\displaystyle \varepsilon \log \varepsilon }$, et cetera. As in the above example, we will obtain outer and inner expansions with some coefficients which must be determined by matching.[6]

## Second-order differential equations

### Schrödinger-like second-order differential equations

A method of matched asymptotic expansions - with matching of solutions in the common domain of validity - has been developed and used extensively by Dingle and Müller-Kirsten for the derivation of asymptotic expansions of the solutions and characteristic numbers (band boundaries) of Schrödinger-like second-order differential equations with periodic potentials - in particular for the Mathieu equation[7] (best example), Lamé and ellipsoidal wave equations,[8] oblate[9] and prolate[10] spheroidal wave equations, and equations with anharmonic potentials.[11]

### Convection-diffusion equations

Methods of matched asymptotic expansions have been developed to find approximate solutions to the Smoluchowski convection-diffusion equation, which is a singularly perturbed second-order differential equation. The problem has been studied particularly in the context of colloid particles in linear flow fields, where the variable is given by the pair distribution function around a test particle. In the limit of low Péclet number, the convection-diffusion equation also presents a singularity at infinite distance (where normally the far-field boundary condition should be placed) due to the flow field being linear in the interparticle separation. This problem can be circumvented with a spatial Fourier transform as shown by Jan Dhont.[12] A different approach to solving this problem was developed by Alessio Zaccone and coworkers and consists in placing the boundary condition right at the boundary layer distance, upon assuming (in a first-order approximation) a constant value of the pair distribution function in the outer layer due to convection being dominant there. This leads to an approximate theory for the encounter rate of two interacting colloid particles in a linear flow field in good agreement with the full numerical solution.[13] When the Péclet number is significantly larger than one, the singularity at infinite separation no longer occurs and the method of matched asymptotics can be applied to construct the full solution for the pair distribution function across the entire domain.[14][15]

#include <bits/stdc++.h>
using namespace std;

using ll = long long;

const ll MOD = 1e9 + 7;
const ll BASE = 911382629;
const int MAXN = 1e5 + 10;

ll base_pows[MAXN * 2], inv_base_pows[MAXN * 2];
ll inv_base;

struct Fenwick {
    vector<ll> tree;
    Fenwick(int n) : tree(n + 2, 0) {}
    void update(int idx, ll delta) {
        for (; idx < tree.size(); idx += idx & -idx)
            tree[idx] = (tree[idx] + delta) % MOD;
    }
    ll query(int idx) {
        ll res = 0;
        for (; idx > 0; idx -= idx & -idx)
            res = (res + tree[idx]) % MOD;
        return res;
    }
};

void precompute(int n) {
    base_pows[0] = 1;
    for (int i = 1; i <= 2 * n; ++i)
        base_pows[i] = base_pows[i - 1] * BASE % MOD;

    inv_base = modpow(BASE, MOD - 2, MOD);
    inv_base_pows[0] = 1;
    for (int i = 1; i <= 2 * n; ++i)
        inv_base_pows[i] = inv_base_pows[i - 1] * inv_base % MOD;
}

int main() {
    ios_base::sync_with_stdio(false);
    cin.tie(nullptr);

    string s;
    cin >> s;
    int n = s.size();
    precompute(n);

    Fenwick hash_tree(n);
    vector<Fenwick> char_trees;
    for (int i = 0; i < 26; ++i)
        char_trees.emplace_back(n);

    for (int i = 1; i <= n; ++i) {
        char c = s[i - 1];
        ll val = (c - 'a' + 1) * base_pows[n - i] % MOD;
        hash_tree.update(i, val);
        char_trees[c - 'a'].update(i, 1);
    }

    int q;
    cin >> q;
    while (q--) {
        int type;
        cin >> type;
        if (type == 1) {
            int idx;
            char ch;
            cin >> idx >> ch;
            char old_c = s[idx - 1];
            s[idx - 1] = ch;

            ll old_val = (old_c - 'a' + 1) * base_pows[n - idx] % MOD;
            ll new_val = (ch - 'a' + 1) * base_pows[n - idx] % MOD;
            hash_tree.update(idx, (new_val - old_val + MOD) % MOD);

            char_trees[old_c - 'a'].update(idx, -1);
            char_trees[ch - 'a'].update(idx, 1);
        } else {
            int l, r;
            string y;
            cin >> l >> r >> y;
            int m = y.size();
            if (m == 1) {
                int c = y[0] - 'a';
                ll ans = char_trees[c].query(r) - char_trees[c].query(l - 1);
                cout << (ans < 0 ? ans + MOD : ans) << '\n';
                continue;
            }
            if (r - l + 1 < m) {
                cout << "0\n";
                continue;
            }
            ll hash_y = 0;
            for (char c : y)
                hash_y = (hash_y * BASE + (c - 'a' + 1)) % MOD;

            int A = l, B = r - m + 1;
            int cnt = 0;
            for (int i = A; i <= B; ++i) {
                int end = i + m - 1;
                ll sub_hash = (hash_tree.query(end) - hash_tree.query(i - 1) + MOD) % MOD;
                sub_hash = sub_hash * inv_base_pows[n - end] % MOD;
                if (sub_hash == hash_y)
                    cnt++;
            }
            cout << cnt << '\n';
        }
    }
    return 0;
}<#sep#>Evaluate the series:

\[
\frac{x}{x+1}+\frac{x^2}{(x+1)(x^2+1)}+\frac{x^4}{(x+1)(x^2+1)(x^4+1)}+\cdots, \;\;\; x>1
\]

<think>
Okay, so I have this series to evaluate: 

\[
\frac{x}{x+1} + \frac{x^2}{(x+1)(x^2+1)} + \frac{x^4}{(x+1)(x^2+1)(x^4+1)} + \cdots
\]

Hmm, let's see. The problem says x is greater than 1, which is probably important for convergence. The series seems to go on with each term adding another factor in the denominator, each being the previous one squared? Wait, the denominators are (x+1), then (x+1)(x²+1), then (x+1)(x²+1)(x⁴+1), etc. So each new denominator multiplies the previous denominator by x raised to the next power of 2 plus 1. Like, the denominators are products of terms of the form \( x^{2^{n}} + 1 \).

Similarly, the numerators are x, x², x⁴, which seem to be x raised to 2^{n} where n starts at 0. Wait, the first term is x, which is x^1, but 2^0 is 1, then 2^1 is 2, then 2^2 is 4, etc. So each numerator is x^{2^{n}} where n starts at 0. So term n (starting from 0) would have numerator x^{2^{n}} and denominator the product from k=0 to n of (x^{2^{k}} + 1).

So the general term of the series is:

\[
\frac{x^{2^{n}}}{\prod_{k=0}^{n} (x^{2^{k}} + 1)}
\]

for n from 0 to infinity.

Hmm, okay. So the series is a sum of terms where each term is x^{2^n} over the product of (x + 1)(x² + 1)...(x^{2^n} + 1). The question is, does this series telescope? Maybe there's a telescoping nature here. Let me try to compute the first few terms and see if I can spot a pattern.

First term: x/(x+1). Let's call this S₀ = x/(x+1).

Second term: x²/[(x+1)(x²+1)]. So S₁ = x/(x+1) + x²/[(x+1)(x²+1)].

Third term adds x⁴/[(x+1)(x²+1)(x⁴+1)].

Wait, maybe if I look at each term, I can express it as a difference between reciprocals or something.

Wait, there's a formula for the product of (x^{2^k} - 1) or something. Wait, in algebra, I remember that the product (x - 1)(x + 1) = x² - 1. Then (x² - 1)(x² + 1) = x⁴ - 1, and so on. So each time you multiply by the next term, you get x^{2^{n}} - 1. Wait, actually:

\[
\prod_{k=0}^{n} (x^{2^k} + 1) = \frac{x^{2^{n+1}} - 1}{x - 1}
\]

Wait, let's check that. For n = 0: (x + 1) = (x² - 1)/(x - 1) = (x - 1)(x + 1)/(x - 1) = x + 1. So that works. For n=1: (x + 1)(x² + 1) = (x⁴ - 1)/(x - 1). Because x⁴ -1 factors into (x² -1)(x² +1) = (x -1)(x +1)(x² +1). So dividing by (x -1) gives (x +1)(x² +1), which matches. So yes, in general, the product from k=0 to n of (x^{2^k} + 1) is (x^{2^{n+1}} -1)/(x -1). Therefore, the denominator of each term in the series is (x^{2^{n+1}} -1)/(x -1). So then each term can be written as:

\[
\frac{x^{2^n}}{\frac{x^{2^{n+1}} - 1}{x - 1}} = \frac{(x -1)x^{2^n}}{x^{2^{n+1}} - 1}
\]

So term n is (x -1)x^{2^n}/(x^{2^{n+1}} -1). So the series becomes:

\[
\sum_{n=0}^{\infty} \frac{(x -1)x^{2^n}}{x^{2^{n+1}} -1}
\]

Hmm, can this be telescoped? Let's see. Maybe each term can be written as a difference between 1/(x^{2^n} -1) and 1/(x^{2^{n+1}} -1). Let me test this.

Suppose we have:

\[
\frac{1}{x^{2^n} - 1} - \frac{1}{x^{2^{n+1}} - 1}
\]

Let's compute this difference:

\[
\frac{1}{x^{2^n} - 1} - \frac{1}{(x^{2^n})^2 - 1} = \frac{1}{x^{2^n} - 1} - \frac{1}{(x^{2^n} -1)(x^{2^n} +1)}
\]

Factor out 1/(x^{2^n} -1):

\[
\frac{1}{x^{2^n} -1} \left(1 - \frac{1}{x^{2^n} +1}\right) = \frac{1}{x^{2^n} -1} \cdot \frac{x^{2^n} +1 -1}{x^{2^n} +1} = \frac{x^{2^n}}{(x^{2^n} -1)(x^{2^n} +1)}
\]

Which simplifies to:

\[
\frac{x^{2^n}}{x^{2^{n+1}} -1}
\]

So, the difference 1/(x^{2^n} -1) - 1/(x^{2^{n+1}} -1) equals x^{2^n}/(x^{2^{n+1}} -1). Hmm, but in our series term, we have (x -1)x^{2^n}/(x^{2^{n+1}} -1). So if we factor out (x -1), each term in the series is (x -1) times [1/(x^{2^n} -1) - 1/(x^{2^{n+1}} -1)].

Therefore, the nth term can be written as:

(x -1) [1/(x^{2^n} -1) - 1/(x^{2^{n+1}} -1)]

Therefore, the series becomes the sum from n=0 to infinity of (x -1) times that difference. Since (x -1) is a constant with respect to n, we can factor it out:

(x -1) * sum_{n=0}^∞ [1/(x^{2^n} -1) - 1/(x^{2^{n+1}} -1)]

But this sum is telescoping! Because when we expand the terms, each negative term cancels with the next positive term.

Let's see:

First term (n=0): (x -1)[1/(x^{1} -1) - 1/(x^{2} -1)]

Second term (n=1): (x -1)[1/(x^{2} -1) - 1/(x^{4} -1)]

Third term (n=2): (x -1)[1/(x^{4} -1) - 1/(x^{8} -1)]

And so on.

When we sum all these terms, the -1/(x² -1) from the first term cancels with the +1/(x² -1) from the second term, the -1/(x⁴ -1) cancels with the next term, and so on<#sep#> can lead to a longer path.

Hamiltonian path is NP-Complete, which is computationally intense. So for even moderately sized maps, this becomes tough.

So the denominator is the sum over all test-cases of (width * height to the power of the term in the big O). Wait the problem says: "your Algorithm has a complexity of O(a*b) Where 'a' is Maze Width and 'b' is Maze Height, then your Algorithm Complexity Total is (TestCase1Width * TestCase1Height) + ... etc." So, in this case, for each test case, the algorithm's complexity is O(a*b) and thus contributes a*b to the total. If the algorithm is, say, O((a*b)^2), then for each test case, contribute (a*b)^2. So, to maximize the score, the algorithm should have as low time complexity as possible (so that denominator is small), while getting the maximum moves (so numerator is large).

Hmm. So perhaps a greedy approach might be better, even if it doesn't find the absolute longest path, but can be done in linear time, rather than trying a brute-force approach which might take exponential time but possibly gets more squares. The challenge is achieving a balance between getting good squares and having low algorithmic complexity.

Hmm.

Alternatively, here's an idea inspired by Tron AIs found online: The classic approach is Breadth-First Search (BFS) to find all possible directions up to a certain look-ahead distance, and then choose the path that leads to the direction with the most possible options (frontier). This is often called the "greedy" approach, or "pseudoforward", because it tries to pick the direction that allows the most space ahead.

The basic idea here is that, when the bike moves, it tries to pick directions such that the next moves have the most "possible next moves". By choosing at each step the direction that maximizes possible future moves, the bike can often find a longer path. This reduces the branching factor and keeps computation manageable.

Let me think step by step:

First, find the current position (the 'p').

Then, for each possible direction to move (the four directions: up, down, left, right), check if that direction is possible (not blocked by a wall or existing trail).

For each possible direction, simulate moving there and then compute the "frontier" — the number of available squares available in that direction, but perhaps using a BFS from there to see how much space is available. But even this could get computationally heavy.

Alternatively, a heuristic for each possible move might be to count the number of available squares adjacent to the current path that can be reached without backtracking, perhaps using BFS to find the connected area reachable from each possible next position, and then choosing the direction that gives the largest reachable area.

Wait, but that seems like it could be O(N) per move step.

Alternatively, here's a possible greedy approach:

At each turn, for each possible adjacent square you can move (i.e., unblocked and unvisited), count the number of possible directions it can move from, excluding where it just came from. The idea is that moving to that next square which has more possible exits might lead to a longer path.

Wait, maybe an even simpler heuristic is to prioritize directions that have the most "corners" or possibilities.

Alternatively, here's another angle: the bike wants to avoid getting into a corner, so at each step, it should choose the direction which allows the next move to have the most options.

So, the heuristic could be: For each direction I can currently move to, look at how many directions I can turn from that new square.

But since sometimes you have to go into a corner, perhaps not.

Alternatively, perhaps a BFS但他 is performed to calculate the available moves from each possible direction. #include <bits/stdc++.h>

using namespace std;

struct Quest {
    int l, m, w;
};

struct State {
    int aL, aM, aW;
    vector<string> choices;
};

using Key = pair<int, int>;
struct StateInfo {
    int sum;
    vector<string> choices;
};

map<Key, StateInfo> process_part(const vector<Quest>& part) {
    vector<State> current_states;
    current_states.push_back({0, 0, 0, {}});

    for (const Quest& q : part) {
        vector<State> new_states;

        for (const State& s : current_states) {
            // LM
            new_states.push_back({
                s.aL + q.l,
                s.aM + q.m,
                s.aW,
                s.choices
            });
            new_states.back().choices.push_back("LM");

            // LW
            new_states.push_back({
                s.aL + q.l,
                s.aM,
                s.aW + q.w,
                s.choices
            });
            new_states.back().choices.push_back("LW");

            // MW
            new_states.push_back({
                s.aL,
                s.aM + q.m,
                s.aW + q.w,
                s.choices
            });
            new_states.back().choices.push_back("MW");
        }

        current_states = move(new_states);
    }

    map<Key, StateInfo> result;

    for (const State& s : current_states) {
        int d1 = s.aL - s.aM;
        int d2 = s.aM - s.aW;
        Key key = {d1, d2};

        int sum = s.aL + s.aM + s.aW;

        auto it = result.find(key);
        if (it == result.end() || sum > it->second.sum) {
            result[key] = {sum, s.choices};
        }
    }

    return result;
}

int main() {
    ios_base::sync_with_stdio(false);
    cin.tie(nullptr);

    int n;
    cin >> n;

    vector<Quest> quests(n);
    for (Quest& q : quests) {
        cin >> q.l >> q.m >> q.w;
    }

    int mid = n / 2;
    vector<Quest> first_part, second_part;
    for (int i = 0; i < mid; ++i) {
        first_part.push_back(quests[i]);
    }
    for (int i = mid; i < n; ++i) {
        second_part.push_back(quests[i]);
    }

    auto first_map = process_part(first_part);
    auto second_map = process_part(second_part);

    int max_total = -1;
    vector<string> best_choices;

    for (const auto& entry : second_map) {
        Key key = entry.first;
        int d1 = key.first;
        int d2 = key.second;

        Key target_key = {-d1, -d2};
        auto it = first_map.find(target_key);
        if (it != first_map.end()) {
            const auto& first_info = it->second;
            const auto& second_info = entry.second;

            int total = first_info.sum + second_info.sum;
            if (total > max_total) {
                max_total = total;
                best_choices = first_info.choices;
                best_choices.insert(best_choices.end(),
                                    second_info.choices.begin(),
                                    second_info.choices.end());
            }
        }
    }

    if (max_total == -1) {
        cout << "Impossible\n";
    } else {
        for (const string& s : best_choices) {
            cout << s << '\n';
        }
    }

    return 0;
}<#sep#> -1)]

Second term (n=1): (x -1)[1/(x^{2} -1) - 1/(x^{4} -1)]

Third term (n=2): (x -1)[1/(x^{4} -1) - 1/(x^{8} -1)]

And so on.

When we sum all these terms, the -1/(x² -1) from the first term cancels with the +1/(x² -1) from the second term, the -1/(x⁴ -1) cancels with the next term, and so on. So the entire sum telescopes to:

(x -1)[1/(x -1) - lim_{n→∞} 1/(x^{2^{n+1}} -1)]

Because all the middle terms cancel out, leaving the first term of the first expression and the limit of the last negative term as n approaches infinity.

Now, we need to evaluate the limit as n approaches infinity of 1/(x^{2^{n+1}} -1). Since x > 1, x^{2^{n+1}} grows exponentially as n increases, so x^{2^{n+1}} approaches infinity, hence 1/(x^{2^{n+1}} -1) approaches 0.

Therefore, the sum simplifies to:

(x -1)[1/(x -1) - 0] = (x -1)(1/(x -1)) = 1.

Therefore, the sum of the series is 1.

Wait, let me check that again. The telescoping sum gives (x -1) multiplied by [1/(x -1) - lim_{n→∞} 1/(x^{2^{n+1}} -1)].

But since the limit term is 0, we have (x -1)*(1/(x -1)) = 1. So the total sum is 1.

Let me verify with a specific value of x. Let's pick x = 2.

Compute the first few terms and see if they approach 1.

First term: 2/(2+1) = 2/3 ≈ 0.6667

Second term: 2²/(2+1)(2²+1) = 4/(3*5) = 4/15 ≈ 0.2667. Total so far: 0.9333.

Third term: 2⁴/(3*5*17) = 16/(255) ≈ 0.0627. Total: ~0.996.

Fourth term: 2^8/(3*5*17*257) = 256/(3*5*17*257) = 256/(65535) ≈ 0.0039. Total: ~1.000.

So after four terms, it's already very close to 1. So that seems to check out. Therefore, the sum converges to 1 when x=2. That's consistent with our result.

Therefore, the answer should be 1, so boxed as \boxed{1}.
</think>To evaluate the series

\[
\frac{x}{x+1} + \frac{x^2}{(x+1)(x^2+1)} + \frac{x^4}{(x+1)(x^2+1)(x^4+1)} + \cdots,
\]

we start by expressing the general term of the series. The \(n\)-th term can be written as:

\[
\frac{x^{2^n}}{\prod_{k=0}^{n} (x^{2^k} + 1)}.
\]

We recognize that the product in the denominator can be simplified using the identity:

\[
\prod_{k=0}^{n} (x^{2^k} + 1) = \frac{x^{2^{n+1}} - 1}{x - 1}.
\]

Thus, the \(n\)-th term becomes:

\[
\frac{x^{2^n}}{\frac{x^{2^{n+1}} - 1}{x - 1}} = \frac{(x - 1)x^{2^n}}{x^{2^{n+1}} - 1}.
\]

We can rewrite this term as:

\[
\frac{(x - 1)x^{2^n}}{x^{2^{n+1}} - 1} = (x - 1) \left( \frac{1}{x^{2^n} - 1} - \frac{1}{x^{2^{n+1}} - 1} \right).
\]

Now, we sum the series from \(n = 0\) to \(\infty\):

\[
\sum_{n=0}^{\infty} \frac{(x - 1)x^{2^n}}{x^{2^{n+1}} - 1} = (x - 1) \sum_{n=0}^{\infty} \left( \frac{1}{x^{2^n} - 1} - \frac{1}{x^{2^{n+1}} - 1} \right).
\]

This series is telescoping. When expanded, the terms cancel out as follows:

\[
(x - 1) \left( \left( \frac{1}{x - 1} - \frac{1}{x^2 - 1} \right) + \left( \frac{1}{x^2 - 1} - \frac{1}{x^4 - 1} \right) + \left( \frac{1}{x^4 - 1} - \frac{1}{x^8 - 1} \right) + \cdots \right).
\]

All intermediate terms cancel, leaving:

\[
(x - 1) \left( \frac{1}{x - 1} - \lim_{n \to \infty} \frac{1}{x^{2^{n+1}} - 1} \right).
\]

Since \(x > 1\), as \(n \to \infty\), \(x^{2^{n+1}} \to \infty\), making the limit term 0. Therefore, the expression simplifies to:

\[
(x - 1) \left( \frac{1}{x - 1} \right) = 1.
\]

Thus, the sum of the series is:

\[
\boxed{1}.
\]<#sep#> square must be empty and not wall). The previous direction doesn't affect the next direction's choice, but the trail that the bike is leaving.

Wait the Tron bike leaves a trail behind, so each movement adds a block. So, the bike cannot go into any square except the current direction's next cell, and the next cell must be empty and not a wall. So, the bike's next direction can be any of the four, but the path has to be such that after moving into that direction, it doesn’t run into itself.

Or does the maze Have walls (the #) and the bike's own path is part of the maze's marks? The problem says, "the map contains a single tron bike, that may already be partially filled". The starting position is 'p', but the other spaces are either empty (space), filled (but by the bike's path) as the game proceeds, or walls ('#').

Hmm, perhaps the bike moves into the next cell, and that becomes part of the trail (the path). So each step, you move one step in some direction, and that square is then filled (so can't revisit it again). The program must decide the path to take, choosing directions each step.

The key is that after each step, the next move mustn't take you into a wall or any already filled square (including previous trail).

So the algorithm's task is to choose a path through the maze that maximizes the total length. The code has to choose the right sequence of moves.

Now, for the algorithm's approach, given that this is a path to follow from the starting point, and given that the optimal path is the longest path possible.

But longest path in a grid graph with some blocked nodes (walls) is what we need.

So, how computationally intensive can this get?

For something like the Complicated test case given, which is a 7x7 grid (approximately?), it's manageable with depth-first search. But for larger mazes, like say 1000x1000 grids, this would be impossible.

The problem's Scoring section mentions an algorithmic complexity that must be considered. Therefore, an O(N^2) algorithm is better than O(2^N), but how can the optimal path be found in O(N^2)? Maybe through dynamic programming?

Hmm.

Dynamic Programming Approach:

We can model this as a state (current position, directions taken, etc.), but that's again too much.

Alternatively, perhaps represent the state as the current position and which squares are filled. But for the maze with W*H squares, this is way too many states.

Alternative idea inspired by BFS and information on longest path:

The longest path problem can sometimes be solved using BFS with a priority on the longest path so far, using backtracking. But the problem is that it can be O(a*b) if the path can be found through backtracking efficiently.

Alternatively, we can use an approach where we greedily choose the direction that leads to the most options further in, and then proceed in that path, backtracking when a dead end is hit.

Wait, but backtracking would be a path-finding algorithm like Depth-First Search (DFS), but pruning paths that can't be better than existing results. Like, if you already have a path with length L=10, you can prune paths that explore a path with current length + remaining space < L. Though calculating the remaining space is tricky.

Another Idea:

The longest possible path is at most (number of blanks +1) in the maze. We could perform a BFS or DFS, keeping track of the path's length, and whenever a path reaches a maximum possible value, we can return it. But even this would take exponential time in the worst case.

Hmm, given the problem constraints, maybe the test cases are small enough that a simple backtracking approach can work, but the problem says the code must work "competitively on a random valid input," so it must be efficient enough for potentially larger mazes.

Alternatively, since the problem is to fill as much as possible, we can use heuristic search like A* star with a good heuristic that estimates the remaining possible squares.

The A* may be a good fit. For each state (current position, and set of visited squares), the heuristic function would estimate the remaining possible maximum path length from that position. The exact maximum is hard, but perhaps an upper bound heuristic.

Wait but the problem is the state representation here.

Alternatively, since the visited squares are important, but remembering all of them is impossible for big mazes, maybe a simpler approach.

Wait, perhaps the path can be tracked as a trail, so the current position and the previous direction are not required, but the path's choices are distance-based.

Wait, the critical thing is that every time you move, the trail 'closes' that square in the map for all future steps. The problem is the shape of the maze's walls and the already placed walls. So, to track all possible states efficiently is tough.

Heuristic: The maximum possible remaining squares plus the current path length.

The A* algorithm chooses which paths to explore based on f = g + h, where g is the current path length, and h is the heuristic of remaining possible squares.

The heuristic h must not overestimate the possible remaining steps, so an admissible heuristic. For example, the maximum possible squares you can reach in the remaining space, ignoring the filled path so far. So, given the current position, the possible h is the number of empty cells reachable from current position without going through filled cells. But that could be an overestimate.

Wait, no, the remaining filled cells would form part of the trail, so the remaining path must be a connected path that goes through unvisited (i.e., not yet part of the path) cells connected to current position. So the heuristic can be the maximum possible steps from the current position in the remaining maze (without walls or filled cells), but even that requires knowing the possible path, which is what we're trying to compute.

Alternatively, the heuristic could be the number of unfilled squares adjacent to the current square, plus 1? Not sure. Hmm.

Alternatively, perhaps the greedy algorithm that picks the direction with the most possible options available (i.e., the direction with the most forward possibilities. For each possible direction, count the number of unvisited squares in the path of any direction from that direction.

Alternatively, thinking of it as, when choosing the next direction, choose the direction that leads to the next position whose "surrounding" (unblocked and not yet filled) is the largest. So the idea is to stay in areas where you can explore more squares. That might lead to longer paths.

#include <vector>
#include <queue>
#include <algorithm>
#include <climits>
#include <string>
#include <cstdio>
using namespace std;

struct Edge {
    int to, rev, capacity;
    long long cost;
};

vector<vector<Edge>> graph;
vector<long long> potential;

void add_edge(int from, int to, int capacity, long long cost) {
    graph[from].push_back(Edge{to, (int)graph[to].size(), capacity, cost});
    graph[to].push_back(Edge{from, (int)graph[from].size() - 1, 0, -cost});
}

pair<long long, int> min_cost_flow(int source, int sink, int max_flow) {
    int n = graph.size();
    potential.assign(n, 0);
    long long total_cost = 0;
    int flow = 0;
    while (flow < max_flow) {
        vector<long long> dist(n, LLONG_MAX);
        vector<int> prev_node(n, -1), prev_edge(n, -1);
        dist[source] = 0;
        priority_queue<pair<long long, int>, vector<pair<long long, int>>, greater<>> pq;
        pq.emplace(0, source);
        while (!pq.empty()) {
            auto [d, u] = pq.top();
            pq.pop();
            if (d > dist[u]) continue;
            for (int i = 0; i < graph[u].size(); ++i) {
                Edge &e = graph[u][i];
                if (e.capacity > 0) {
                    int v = e.to;
                    long long new_dist = d + e.cost + potential[u] - potential[v];
                    if (new_dist < dist[v]) {
                        dist[v] = new_dist;
                        prev_node[v] = u;
                        prev_edge[v] = i;
                        pq.emplace(new_dist, v);
                    }
                }
            }
        }
        if (dist[sink] == LLONG_MAX) break;
        for (int i = 0; i < n; ++i)
            if (dist[i] < LLONG_MAX)
                potential[i] += dist[i];
        int augment = max_flow - flow;
        for (int v = sink; v != source; v = prev_node[v]) {
            int u = prev_node[v];
            Edge &e = graph[u][prev_edge[v]];
            augment = min(augment, e.capacity);
        }
        flow += augment;
        total_cost += augment * (potential[sink] - potential[source]);
        for (int v = sink; v != source; v = prev_node[v]) {
            int u = prev_node[v];
            Edge &e = graph[u][prev_edge[v]];
            e.capacity -= augment;
            graph[v][e.rev].capacity += augment;
        }
    }
    return {total_cost, flow};
}

int main() {
    int n, m;
    scanf("%d %d", &n, &m);
    vector<string> s(n);
    for (int i = 0; i < n; ++i) {
        char buf[21];
        scanf("%s", buf);
        s[i] = buf;
    }
    vector<vector<int>> a(n, vector<int>(m));
    for (int i = 0; i < n; ++i) {
        for (int j = 0; j < m; ++j) {
            scanf("%d", &a[i][j]);
        }
    }
    int num_pairs = m * 26;
    int sink_node = n + num_pairs + 1;
    graph.resize(sink_node + 1);
    // Source to strings
    for (int i = 0; i < n; ++i) {
        add_edge(0, i + 1, 1, 0);
    }
    // (j, c) pairs to sink
    for (int j = 0; j < m; ++j) {
        for (char c = 'a'; c <= 'z'; ++c) {
            int pair_index = j * 26 + (c - 'a') + n + 1;
            add_edge(pair_index, sink_node, 1, 0);
        }
    }
    // Strings to (j, c) pairs
    for (int i = 0; i < n; ++i) {
        for (int j = 0; j < m; ++j) {
            char original = s[i][j];
            for (char c = 'a'; c <= 'z'; ++c) {
                long long cost = (c == original) ? 0 : a[i][j];
                int pair_index = j * 26 + (c - 'a') + n + 1;
                add_edge(i + 1, pair_index, 1, cost);
            }
        }
    }
    auto [total_cost, flow] = min_cost_flow(0, sink_node, n);
    printf("%lld\n", total_cost);
    return 0;
}<#sep#>In \( \triangle ABC \), \( AB = 10 \), \( AC = 12 \), and \( m \angle B = 2m \angle A \). If \( A \) is the smallest angle in \( \triangle ABC \), compute \( BC \).

<think>
Okay, so I need to solve this triangle problem where in triangle ABC, AB is 10 units, AC is 12 units, and the measure of angle B is twice the measure of angle A. Also, angle A is the smallest angle in the triangle. I have to find the length of BC. Hmm, let's start by recalling some triangle theorems that might help here.

First, I remember the Law of Sines, which relates the sides of a triangle to the sines of their opposite angles. The formula is (a/sin A) = (b/sin B) = (c/sin C). Maybe that could be useful here since we have two sides and some relationship between the angles.

Given that angle B is twice angle A, let's denote angle A as α. Then angle B would be 2α. Since the sum of the angles in a triangle is 180 degrees, angle C would be 180° - α - 2α = 180° - 3α. But the problem also states that angle A is the smallest angle. That makes sense because angle B is twice angle A, and angle C would be 180° - 3α. So angle C must be larger than angle B unless 3α is greater than some amount. Wait, let's check that.

If angle A is the smallest, then angles B and C must be larger. Since angle B is 2α, which is already twice angle A, angle C would be 180° - 3α. To ensure that angle C is larger than angle A and angle B, we need:

180° - 3α > 2α
180° > 5α
α < 36°

So angle A must be less than 36 degrees. That seems okay. Let's note that down.

Now, in terms of sides, AB is 10, AC is 12. Let's make sure we label the triangle correctly. In triangle ABC, the side opposite angle A is BC, the side opposite angle B is AC, and the side opposite angle C is AB. Wait, let me confirm:

- Side opposite angle A is BC.
- Side opposite angle B is AC.
- Side opposite angle C is AB.

So, according to the Law of Sines:

BC / sin A = AC / sin B = AB / sin C

Given that AB is 10, AC is 12. So, substituting the known sides:

BC / sin α = 12 / sin(2α) = 10 / sin(180° - 3α)

But sin(180° - 3α) is equal to sin(3α), since sine is positive in the second quadrant and sin(180° - x) = sin x.

So, simplifying, we have:

BC / sin α = 12 / sin(2α) = 10 / sin(3α)

Hmm. Maybe we can set up equations here. Let's take the first two ratios:

BC / sin α = 12 / sin(2α)

We can solve for BC here. Since sin(2α) = 2 sin α cos α, substituting that in:

BC / sin α = 12 / (2 sin α cos α)

Multiply both sides by sin α:

BC = 12 / (2 cos α) = 6 / cos α

So BC = 6 / cos α. Got that. Now, let's take another ratio. Let's take the second and third terms:

12 / sin(2α) = 10 / sin(3α)

Again, substitute sin(2α) as 2 sin α cos α and sin(3α) using the sine triple angle formula. Hmm, sin(3α) can be written as 3 sin α - 4 sin³ α. Wait, but maybe there's a better way. Alternatively, use the identity sin(3α) = sin(2α + α) = sin 2α cos α + cos 2α sin α.

So sin(3α) = sin 2α cos α + cos 2α sin α. Let me compute that:

= (2 sin α cos α) cos α + (1 - 2 sin² α) sin α

= 2 sin α cos² α + sin α - 2 sin³ α

= sin α (2 cos² α + 1 - 2 sin² α)

But 2 cos² α can be written as 2(1 - sin² α), so substituting:

= sin α [2(1 - sin² α) + 1 - 2 sin² α]

= sin α [2 - 2 sin² α + 1 - 2 sin² α]

= sin α [3 - 4 sin² α]

Hmm, that's the same as the standard formula for sin(3α) = 3 sin α - 4 sin³ α. So that's correct.

Alternatively, maybe we can use the ratio equation:

12 / sin(2α) = 10 / sin(3α)

Cross-multiplying:

12 sin(3α) = 10 sin(2α)

Let me substitute sin(3α) as 3 sin α - 4 sin³ α and sin(2α) as 2 sin α cos α:

12 (3 sin α - 4 sin³ α) = 10 * 2 sin α cos α

Simplify:

36 sin α - 48 sin³ α = 20 sin α cos α

Let's move all terms to one side:

36 sin α - 48 sin³ α - 20 sin α cos α = 0

Factor out sin α:

sin α (36 - 48 sin² α - 20 cos α) = 0

So either sin α = 0, which is impossible since α is an angle in a triangle and must be between 0 and 180°, or:

36 - 48 sin² α - 20 cos α = 0

Hmm, this seems a bit messy. Maybe there's a better way. Let's think.

Alternatively, we can divide both sides of the equation 12 sin(3α) = 10 sin(2α) by sin α (since sin α ≠ 0):

12 (3 - 4 sin² α) = 10 * 2 cos α

Because sin(3α)/sin α = 3 - 4 sin² α (from the expression above) and sin(2α)/sin α = 2 cos α.

So:

12*(3 - 4 sin² α) = 20 cos α

Divide both sides by 4:

3*(3 - 4 sin² α) = 5 cos α

9 - 12 sin² α = 5 cos α

Now, since sin² α = 1 - cos² α, substitute that:

9 - 12 (1 - cos² α) = 5 cos α

Simplify:

9 - 12 + 12 cos² α = 5 cos α

-3 + 12 cos² α = 5 cos α

Bring all terms to left side:

12 cos² α - 5 cos α - 3 = 0

Now, this is a quadratic in terms of cos α. Let's set x = cos α:

12x² -5x -3 = 0

Solving for x:

x = [5 ± √(25 + 144)] / 24

Because discriminant D = (-5)^2 -4*12*(-3) = 25 + 144 = 169

So,

x = [5 ± 13]/24

So two solutions:

x = (5 +13)/24 = 18/24 = 3/4

x = (5 -13)/24 = (-8)/24 = -1/3

Since α is an angle in a triangle, and angle A is the smallest angle, which is less than 36°, as we found earlier. So cos α must be positive because angles less than 90° have positive cosine. So the negative solution is invalid here. Therefore, cos α = 3/4.

Therefore, cos α = 3/4. Then, BC = 6 / cos α = 6 / (3/4) = 6 * (4/3) = 8.

Wait, so BC would be 8. Is that the answer?

Let me check this again.

We found cos α = 3/4, so α = arccos(3/4). Let's compute angle α approximately to see if angle C is indeed larger than angle B. So arccos(3/4) is approximately 41.4 degrees, but wait, earlier we said that α must be less than 36°<#sep#> in each step, the direction that maximizes the available options would be chosen.

Perhaps choosing at each turn the direction which gives the highest count of possible next steps. For instance, when at the end of the corridor (after going right four times), the next possible step are the options down or perhaps left? But moving left from the end of the corridor is allowed? Let's see.

Wait, after four rights, you're at column 4 (if starting at 0). The next move to the right would hit a wall, so that direction is blocked. Similarly, down might be an option, then moving down would allow the path to go down and right again?

Hmm.

In any case, the greedy approach needs to pick directions such that you have more exits in your future steps. So, when in the corridor moving right, the next steps have high possibilities, so the first moves are in that direction until you can't go any further, but perhaps at that point, turning down would lead to a traversal path.

Another idea: Use BFS to explore all possible paths, but backtrack when you hit a terminal state. However, with pruning. Since the problem only requires the best path (longest possible path), we can keep track of the current best path and backtrack only when the remaining steps could better it.

This approach might be feasible for smaller mazes but would be too slow for large ones. However, if it's efficient enough and the problem's test cases are reasonable in size, maybe it can work.

Alternatively, since the maze's walls are fixed (input), and the path must be a single trail, perhaps each unique position can be associated with a direction (or not) chosen so far. But again, with the path being a single line, perhaps the next step can only go away from the trail, so in some way, it can be represented as positions and direction, but I'm not sure.

Hmm. Let's think about a simple implementation. For each position, and for each possible path leading there, track the path length so far and direction recent (since direction can matter when you can go back?), but actually, in this problem, the only requirement is that you can't move into a visited square or a wall, which is kept track of as checked positions. 

Thus, each state consists of:

- current (x,y) coordinates 

- set of visited coordinates (which is the trail up to now). 

But storing the visited coordinates for each path is expensive. So the state space becomes impossible for large grids.

Hmm, this is problematic.

Alternative Idea:

We can represent the maze's visited squares as part of the state, but when branching, we can use a BFS-like approach where each step branched is only tracked as possible if the path is better than prior options. To keep it manageable, perhaps some heuristic is required.

Alternative Approach:

The problem can be modeled as a graph where each node is a (x,y) position, and an edge connects to all possible next positions (next positions must not be walls or previously visited). Wait no, because visited squares are in the path's history, so actually, the edges depend on the path taken, so it's not a static graph.

Alternatively, for each position, the transitions depend on the path taken to reach there. So, the next moves from a position x,y are all directions not blocked by walls nor in the visited set.

Thus, the problem again is that's dynamic.

Another Idea: use memoization where for each position and direction you came from, you can note the maximum path length available from that point. 

Wait, but:

If we think of the path, the direction you came from is important in terms of where you can go next (but actually not needed because you just can't go back? No, Tron can turn in any direction as long as it doesn't hit itself.)

Alternatively: Let's try a recursive backtracking approach, provided that the maze is of manageable size.

But given that the problem requires being competitive for a random maze, recursion may stack overflow beyond a certain depth, so perhaps an iterative implementation.

The recursive approach would proceed as follows:

We can represent the current path as a list of directions, and track the visited squares (as a set of coordinates). At each step, for each of the four directions (north, south, etc.):

- Check if moving that direction is possible (not a wall, not in visited).

- For each possible move, proceed.

- Keep track of the longest path found so far. Whenever a path ends (no options left), we compare it with the current longest.

The problem is that this could explore all possibilities, which is O(3^N) in worst case (since each step, you have 3 choices assuming you can never go back the way you came, which is not true but maybe it's 3?), which is terrible for even N=20.

But maybe we can use pruning to cut down paths that are already shorter than some known maximum. 

But even with pruning, it may not be feasible for larger mazes.

Hmm, but what's the problem said?

"The input will always be an enclosed surrounding the p, there will always be at least 1 space to move from the start, and the area may not always be completely fillable. Your code should work competitively on random valid input, not just the test cases.”

Thus, the author expects this to be solvable with an efficient approach, or at least the best possible heuristics. 

Alternative Idea inspired by BFS for finding the longest path:

At each node (position), we can track the length of the current path and use priority queues. Here's an approach to do that heuristically:

- We can use a priority queue that orders the search by the current longest path.

- The priority queue stores the current path's length and the current location.

- For each step, select the path with the longest length, and then explore all possible directions, which could lead to longer paths.

Wait, but this would be a variant of best-first search.

However, even this can have exponential growth if the paths are long. 

Alternatively, using memoization, for each position, we can keep track of the maximum path length achievable from that position, given the path taken. But the path taken is represented somehow.

Another Idea inspired by the following:  When in a maze, one "good" path is to always move in the direction that allows maximum expansion. The key here is to always pick the direction which gives maximum flexibility in the next step. So, each step's selection can be made based on the current cell's neighbors (unoccupied and not walls). For each next step, check how many directions it can proceed from there. This could be implemented as a greedy approach:

The algorithm would always choose the direction which, after moving into that cell, has the most available neighboring cells (i.e., squares that haven't been visited yet and aren't walls).

For example:

The next step to choose is the direction that would give the next cell with the most neighbors. That way, you're trying to delay getting into a corner as long as possible.

This could maximize the path's length, and it's computationally lightweight:

- At each step, for each adjacent cell that's accessible and not in the maze's walls or current path:

Calculate for each, the number of available adjacent squares (excluding walls and nodes already visited, except for the current path).

Pick the direction with the highest such value. If there's a tie, choose one arbitrarily (or by priority, like preferring a particular direction like down, right, etc.)?

This greedy approach is O(M) per step, where M is the maze area. But since each step only considers the current node's neighbors, this is O(M*N), but each step is O(1) per surrounding four cells. 

Wait, each step requires checking each available direction, then for that cell a neighbor:

To compute the number of available cells around that neighbor.

Wait, let's say that at each step, when considering moving to a new square S, you must first:

1. Check four direction from S: 

   For each direction, check if that cell is not a wall or part of the current trail (excluding the current path except S's position).

Wait, the current trail up to one step before moving to S has already been recorded, so for S's new position, the next step is to mark it as visited. 

Wait the algorithm for this heuristic would do roughly (for each possible direction of possible moves):

For each possible direction:

- see if we can move there (available cell, not wall, and not visited yet)

- then move to that cell, calculate how many of its adjacent cells are available (not walls and not yet visited, except the current path).

- pick the direction with the highest count.

This would require, at each step, to look at the next 4 directions:

For each direction candidate, compute the count of available directions from there.

But this would require for each step, doing for each candidate direction a check of four adjacent squares (except where walls are).

#include <bits/stdc++.h>
using namespace std;

int main() {
    ios::sync_with_stdio(false);
    cin.tie(nullptr);

    int n, r, c;
    cin >> n >> r >> c;
    vector<string> words(n);
    for (auto &s : words) cin >> s;

    vector<bool> valid(n + 1);
    for (int i = 1; i <= n; ++i) {
        valid[i] = (words[i - 1].size() <= c);
    }

    vector<int> next_invalid(n + 2);
    next_invalid[n] = valid[n] ? n + 1 : n;
    for (int i = n - 1; i >= 1; --i) {
        if (!valid[i]) {
            next_invalid[i] = i;
        } else {
            next_invalid[i] = next_invalid[i + 1];
        }
    }

    vector<int> s(n + 1);
    for (int i = 1; i <= n; ++i) {
        s[i] = s[i - 1] + words[i - 1].size();
    }

    vector<int> next_line_end(n + 2);
    for (int i = 1; i <= n; ++i) {
        if (!valid[i]) {
            next_line_end[i] = i - 1;
            continue;
        }
        int max_j = next_invalid[i] - 1;
        if (i > max_j) {
            next_line_end[i] = i - 1;
            continue;
        }
        int lo = i;
        int hi = max_j;
        int best = i - 1;
        while (lo <= hi) {
            int mid = (lo + hi) / 2;
            int sum_words = s[mid] - s[i - 1];
            int total = sum_words + (mid - i);
            if (total <= c) {
                best = mid;
                lo = mid + 1;
            } else {
                hi = mid - 1;
            }
        }
        next_line_end[i] = best;
    }

    const int max_level = 20;
    vector<vector<int>> jump(max_level + 1, vector<int>(n + 2));
    for (int i = 1; i <= n; ++i) {
        jump[0][i] = next_line_end[i] + 1;
    }
    for (int k = 1; k <= max_level; ++k) {
        for (int i = 1; i <= n; ++i) {
            if (jump[k - 1][i] > n) {
                jump[k][i] = n + 1;
            } else {
                jump[k][i] = jump[k - 1][jump[k - 1][i]];
            }
        }
    }

    int max_len = -1;
    int best_i = 0, best_j = 0;
    for (int i = 1; i <= n; ++i) {
        if (!valid[i]) continue;
        int pos = i;
        int rem = r;
        for (int k = max_level; k >= 0; --k) {
            if (rem >= (1 << k)) {
                if (pos > n) break;
                pos = jump[k][pos];
                rem -= (1 << k);
            }
        }
        int j = pos - 1;
        if (pos > n + 1) j = n;
        if (j < i) continue;
        int len = j - i + 1;
        if (len > max_len || (len == max_len && i < best_i)) {
            max_len = len;
            best_i = i;
            best_j = j;
        }
    }

    vector<string> output;
    int current = best_i;
    for (int _ = 0; _ < r; ++_) {
        if (current > best_j) {
            output.emplace_back("");
            continue;
        }
        int end = next_line_end[current];
        if (end > best_j) end = best_j;
        string line;
        for (int i = current; i <= end; ++i) {
            if (i != current) line += ' ';
            line += words[i - 1];
        }
        output.push_back(line);
        current = end + 1;
    }

    int printed = 0;
    for (const auto &line : output) {
        if (printed >= r) break;
        if (!line.empty()) {
            cout << line << '\n';
            printed++;
        }
    }

    return 0;

</think>To solve for the length of \( BC \) in \( \triangle ABC \) where \( AB = 10 \), \( AC = 12 \), and \( m \angle B = 2m \angle A \), and \( \angle A \) is the smallest angle, we can use the Law of Sines and trigonometric identities.

1. **Define the angles:**
   Let \( \angle A = \alpha \). Then \( \angle B = 2\alpha \) and \( \angle C = 180^\circ - 3\alpha \).

2. **Apply the Law of Sines:**
   \[
   \frac{BC}{\sin \alpha} = \frac{AC}{\sin 2\alpha} = \frac{AB}{\sin (180^\circ - 3\alpha)}
   \]
   Since \( \sin (180^\circ - 3\alpha) = \sin 3\alpha \), we have:
   \[
   \frac{BC}{\sin \alpha} = \frac{12}{\sin 2\alpha} = \frac{10}{\sin 3\alpha}
   \]

3. **Use the double-angle and triple-angle identities:**
   \[
   \sin 2\alpha = 2 \sin \alpha \cos \alpha
   \]
   \[
   \sin 3\alpha = 3 \sin \alpha - 4 \sin^3 \alpha
   \]

4. **Set up the equation using the Law of Sines:**
   \[
   \frac{BC}{\sin \alpha} = \frac{12}{2 \sin \alpha \cos \alpha}
   \]
   Simplifying, we get:
   \[
   BC = \frac{12}{2 \cos \alpha} = \frac{6}{\cos \alpha}
   \]

   \[
   \frac{12}{2 \sin \alpha \cos \alpha} = \frac{10}{3 \sin \alpha - 4 \sin^3 \alpha}
   \]

   \[

- then move to that cell, calculate how many of its adjacent cells are available (not walls and not yet visited, except the current path).

- pick the direction with the highest count.

This would require, at each step, to look at the next 4 directions:

For each direction candidate, compute the count of available directions from there.

But this would require for each step, doing for each candidate direction a check of four adjacent squares (except where walls are).

So for each step, operations O(1) since it's 4(possible directions) each with checking up to 4 cells (4 directions from next cell). So 16 checks.

Therefore, even in a maze of 1000x1000 squares, this would take O(N) steps, each with O(1), so O(N) time.

That might be so efficient, and the greedy approach might be the way to go here.

Even if it might not find the optimal path, it would be O(N), and the competition requires maximizing the accumulated path length divided by algorithmic complexity (O(a*b)), so for a maze of m cells, a = rows, b = cols. The algorithm's execution is O(m) but the Alg complexity is O(a*b) if it's O(m). Since m = a*b, so it's O(ab), leading to denominator being Sum(a_i*b_i). The numerator would be the number of moves actually taken. 

So it's possible that this greedy approach would do well in terms of the score.

Another idea: choose the direction which maximizes the "available space" that can be reached from there. This requires calculating for each possible move direction, the reachable unreached squares count starting from that next position. But that's more computationally expensive.

Calculating the reachable squares at each step may be done via a BFS from the next cell, without considering the path taken, since walls and visited squares (apart from the current path) would be the maze's walls plus all existing path up to that step.

Wait but this is tricky because the visited set is the current path itself. To compute the reachable area from a new position after moving there would require considering all squares not yet visited or walls.

So, to do this:

For each possible direction D from current position:

- Position (x,y) is current position.

- The new position is (x+dx[D], y+dy[D]).

- Check if that new position is possible.

If it is, then simulate moving there, and count the area you can reach from that new position, excluding current path expanded to include this new cell.

So:

- the new cell is added to a temporary path list, then run BFS or grid flood from there. Not counting the temporary path (except that the path is now up to this point).

But this requires for each candidate move direction, a BFS of the map.

For a map with N squares, this is O(D*M*M) time, where D is the number of directions (4). For N=1000, it's ~4,000,000 per step. For step counts of 1000, this would be 4 billion operations.

That's too slow for larger mazes.

Therefore, the first greedy idea of looking at the next cell's options seems better for efficiency.

Now, trying to proceed with the greedy approach:

The algorithm steps:

Initialize:

- Start position: find 'p' in the maze.

- Mark current position as visited (add to visited set).

- The path is initially empty (though we'll build it by appending directions).

- Directions: for each step, pick an eligible direction (not blocked, not visited) and choose the one with the highest "score" (number of available cells around the next position).

- Repeat until no directions are available.

Edge case: if there are multiple directions to choose with same score: favor one, perhaps randomly? But maybe when in competition mode, choosing the direction which allows the longest path would depend on this choice. To keep algorithms deterministic, the tie-breaker could be selecting in the order of right, down, left, up (or any order, like priority order R, D, L, U?).

Wait, but to maximize the path length, it's better to have an ordering that explores options that have more possibilities next. So for the tied case, perhaps choose one arbitrarily, but we can pick a direction that has a higher absolute score of deeper paths.

But without backtracking, greedily choosing the direction with the highest current score is the best we can do.

So the algorithm in steps:

Initialize:

- Find the starting position (p) in the maze grid.

- Create a visited matrix or set that starts with the starting position.

- Current position is (x,y).

- The path is a string starting as empty.

Loop until no possible moves:

1. For each of the four directions (R, U, L, D):

a. compute dx, dy according to direction (e.g., R: (1,0), U(0,-1) assuming rows are y-axis).

temp<#sep#>Avoid trying to order an OFFLINE product
From @remi-braun in https://github.com/CS-SI/eodag/issues/214

> Maybe add the LTA order as a parameter, as it is not needed in every usecase (sometimes, sb just want to download everything he can without triggering any LTA retrieval)

LTA: Long Term Archive

The `HTTPDownload` plugin (used by creodias, onda, theia, peps, sobloo) can automatically try to order a product if its status is *OFFLINE*, and can download it afterwards.

The feature request is to add a way to avoid trying to order an *OFFLINE* product.

This could be done in at least two complementary ways:
1. Document the way to filter out *OFFLINE* products from the result of a search. This can be done as follows currently:
```python
from eodag.plugins.crunch.filter_property import FilterProperty
dag.set_preferred_provider("peps")
prods, _ = dag.search(...)
online_prods = prods.crunch(FilterProperty({"storageStatus": "ONLINE", "operator": "eq"}))
dag.download_all(online_prods)
```
2. Add a parameter to pass as a kwarg to `EODataAccessGateway.download_all` (not sure it's relevant for `download`), e.g. `skip_offline`, that would be passed to the HTTPPlugin and, if `True`, would stop trying to download this product, which would lead to `download_all` trying to download the next product.

#include <bits/stdc++.h>
using namespace std;

struct DSU {
    vector<int> parent;
    vector<int> size;
    vector<int> diameter;

    DSU(int n) {
        parent.resize(n + 1);
        size.resize(n + 1, 1);
        diameter.resize(n + 1, 0);
        for (int i = 1; i <= n; ++i) {
            parent[i] = i;
        }
    }

    int find(int u) {
        if (parent[u] != u) {
            parent[u] = find(parent[u]);
        }
        return parent[u];
    }

    void unite(int u, int v) {
        u = find(u);
        v = find(v);
        if (u == v) return;
        if (size[u] < size[v]) {
            swap(u, v);
        }
        parent[v] = u;
        size[u] += size[v];
        int d1 = diameter[u];
        int d2 = diameter[v];
        int r1 = (d1 + 1) / 2;
        int r2 = (d2 + 1) / 2;
        diameter[u] = max({d1, d2, r1 + r2 + 1});
    }
};

pair<int, int> bfs(int start, const vector<vector<int>>& adj) {
    queue<pair<int, int>> q; // node, parent
    unordered_map<int, int> dist;
    q.push({start, -1});
    dist[start] = 0;
    int max_dist = 0;
    int far_node = start;

    while (!q.empty()) {
        auto [u, parent] = q.front();
        q.pop();

        for (int v : adj[u]) {
            if (v != parent) {
                if (!dist.count(v)) {
                    dist[v] = dist[u] + 1;
                    if (dist[v] > max_dist) {
                        max_dist = dist[v];
                        far_node = v;
                    }
                    q.push({v, u});
                }
            }
        }
    }

    return {far_node, max_dist};
}

int main() {
    ios_base::sync_with_stdio(false);
    cin.tie(nullptr);

    int n, m, q;
    cin >> n >> m >> q;

    vector<vector<int>> adj(n + 1);
    DSU dsu(n);

    for (int i = 0; i < m; ++i) {
        int a, b;
        cin >> a >> b;
        adj[a].push_back(b);
        adj[b].push_back(a);
        dsu.unite(a, b);
    }

    map<int, vector<int>> components;
    for (int i = 1; i <= n; ++i) {
        components[dsu.find(i)].push_back(i);
    }

    for (auto& [root, nodes] : components) {
        vector<vector<int>> component_adj(n + 1);
        for (int u : nodes) {
            for (int v : adj[u]) {
                if (dsu.find(v) == root) {
                    component_adj[u].push_back(v);
                }
            }
        }

        if (nodes.size() == 1) {
            dsu.diameter[root] = 0;
            continue;
        }

        auto [u, d1] = bfs(nodes[0], component_adj);
        auto [v, d2] = bfs(u, component_adj);
        dsu.diameter[root] = d2;
    }

    while (q--) {
        int type;
        cin >> type;
        if (type == 1) {
            int x;
            cin >> x;
            int root = dsu.find(x);
            cout << dsu.diameter[root] << '\n';
        } else {
            int x, y;
            cin >> x >> y;
            dsu.unite(x, y);
        }
    }

    return 0;

   \[
   18 \sin \alpha - 24 \sin^3 \alpha = 10 \cos \alpha
   \]
   Divide by 2:
   \[
   9 \sin \alpha - 12 \sin^3 \alpha = 5 \cos \alpha
   \]

7. **Express \(\sin \alpha\) and \(\cos \alpha\) in terms of a single variable:**
   Let \( x = \cos \alpha \). Then \( \sin \alpha = \sqrt{1 - x^2} \). Substitute:
   \[
   9 \sqrt{1 - x^2} - 12 (1 - x^2) \sqrt{1 - x^2} = 5x
   \]
   Simplify:
   \[
   9 \sqrt{1 - x^2} - 12 (1 - x^2) \sqrt{1 - x^2} = 5x
   \]
   \[
   9 \sqrt{1 - x^2} - 12 \sqrt{1 - x^2} + 12x^2 \sqrt{1 - x^2} = 5x
   \]
   \[
   -3 \sqrt{1 - x^2} + 12x^2 \sqrt{1 - x^2} = 5x
   \]
   Factor out \(\sqrt{1 - x^2}\):
   \[
   \sqrt{1 - x^2} (-3 + 12x^2) = 5x
   \]
   Square both sides:
   \[
   (1 - x^2) (144x^4 - 72x^2 + 9) = 25x^2
   \]
   \[
   144x^4 - 72x^2 + 9 - 144x^6 + 72x^4 - 9x^2 = 25x^2
   \]
   \[
   -144x^6 + 216x^4 - 106x^2 + 9 = 0
   \]
   Let \( y = x^2 \):
   \[
   -144y^3 + 216y^2 - 106y + 9 = 0
   \]
   Solve this cubic equation using numerical methods or factorization to find \( y \approx \frac{9}{16} \).

8. **Find \( \cos \alpha \):**
   \[
   \cos \alpha = \sqrt{\frac{9}{16}} = \frac{3}{4}
   \]

9. **Calculate \( BC \):**
   \[
   BC = \frac{6}{\cos \alpha} = \frac{6}{\frac{3}{4}} = 8
   \]

Bug linked to #423. 
Sorry to reopen again this issue, but I have encountered new products where it fails 😅
I am recreating a new issue to be more precise.

**Describe the bug**

Some products are missing a ProductPath in their properties, leading to failure to find the manifest file
`FileNotFoundError: No manifest.safe could be found in D:\_EODOWNLOAD\OUTPUT\GRAND_EST\L2A\S2B_MSIL2A_20211229T130249_N0301_R095_T24MUV_20211229T151639`
![2022-03-16_09h17_21](https://user-images.githubusercontent.com/67311115/158546345-4ce55c5e-d57d-4c8e-afff-25029c837625.png)

This comes from the fact that the ProductPath is missing and its url is replaced by the product location in `get_bucket_name_and_prefix` (in `aws.py`). This leads to a wrong name and bucket:

```python
print(
    self.get_bucket_name_and_prefix(
        product, product.properties["productPath"]
    )
)
    
('v0', '/collections/sentinel-s2-l2a/items/S2A_32TLT_20211016_0_L2A')
```

**Code To Reproduce**
```py

    setup_logging(2)  # DEBUG level

    with tempfile.TemporaryDirectory() as tmp:
        # Create gateway
        dag = EODataAccessGateway()
        dag.set_preferred_provider("earth_search")

        # Query all
        products = dag.search_all(
            start="2021-12-28",
            end="2021-12-30",
            **{
                "productType": "S2_MSI_L2A",
                "geom": "POINT (-40.30881686219417 -5.018666560087038)"
            }
        )

        # Download all
        dag.download_all(products)
```
